And I won't just do that—I'll be doing it while simultaneously making these incredibly complex, multi-tab spreadsheet care plans for my brother's medical needs. And then, while I'm bouncing between those two things, I'm emailing relatives, sending these huge, detailed updates to my aunts and cousins at 4:15 in the morning. Which, in hindsight, I know they probably find a bit alarming to wake up to, seeing timestamps like that, but in the moment, I just have to get the information out there. And I'll be reorganizing the shared digital files for the partners I support, renaming everything, color-coding the folders... just doing it all at once. \n\nIt’s this intense, driving pressure, honestly, like sitting still would mean I’m dropping the ball on everything. If I stop typing, if I stop planning and fixing things, it feels like the whole house of cards is just going to completely collapse. So, no, it's definitely not my normal baseline. My normal baseline always had a stopping point, a moment where I could say, \"Okay, the work is done for today.\" But during these nights, there is no stopping point. I just have to keep all the plates spinning at lightning speed.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling that heaviness and flatness during the day—the exhaustion, the lack of interest in photography and mentoring—how long do those stretches typically last before you start to feel better? And separately, how long do those energized, highly productive periods at night tend to go on?\n\nPatient: Well, the heaviness... gosh, it's so hard to put a strict timeline on the emptiness because when you're in it, time just sort of stops. It just feels endless, like it's stretching out forever and you can't even remember what a normal day feels like. \n\nBut those energized periods? The ones where I'm waking up in the pitch dark? I actually have a very specific timeline for those, because, like I said, I document everything. It's the executive assistant in me, I just can't help but track the data and look for the patterns. I was actually looking through my little nightstand notebook just the other day, cross-referencing it with the timestamps on my sent emails—because you can sort Outlook by time of day, which is an absolute lifesaver when you're managing multiple executives across different time zones—and I realized exactly how long this has been going on. \n\nThese elevated, driven stretches... where I'm functioning on practically zero sleep and just bursting with, frankly, way too much confidence about how I can single-handedly organize everyone's lives and solve every logistical crisis... they've been recurring for about eleven months now. Eleven months! I traced it all the way back to late last year. I remember because I had completely redesigned the firm's holiday travel matrix at 3:15 in the morning and felt like an absolute genius doing it. I was so incredibly sure of myself, just running on pure adrenaline and no rest, thinking I had unlocked some secret level of productivity that nobody else knew about. \n\nSo yes, it's been about eleven months that these specific, highly driven, sleepless stretches have been popping up and recurring. It's just wild to look at the paper trail and see it laid out like that, seeing month after month where I have these bursts of just absolute, over-the-top confidence and energy, completely taking over my nights.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those energized, highly productive stretches—like the one where you redesigned the holiday travel matrix at 3 AM—how many nights in a row does that elevated energy and reduced need for sleep typically last before it stops?\n\nPatient: Gosh, you know, that is the one piece of data that absolutely drives me crazy because... well, you'd think with my little nightstand notebook and my obsession with tracking the timestamps on my Outlook sent folder, I would have a precise number for you. A neat little spreadsheet column that says, you know, \"Stretch duration: exactly this many days.\" \n\nBut... I really don't. I don't actually know how many nights in a row it lasts. \n\nIt’s so incredibly frustrating for me as someone whose entire career is built on knowing exactly how long a quarterly board meeting will run, or exactly how many days a senior partner needs on the ground in Tokyo before catching a flight to London. I live for exact durations. But when I’m in one of those stretches... time just sort of loses its normal structure. It’s like the boundaries between, say, Monday night, Tuesday morning, and Wednesday afternoon just completely dissolve into this one continuous, high-speed blur of doing, doing, doing. \n\nI’ll look back at my notes later trying to piece it together, and I might see I was up at 3 AM on a Tuesday, and then there are frantic scribbles from 4 AM on a Wednesday, but sometimes I forget to log things properly because I’m just so caught up in the momentum of it all—like redesigning that travel matrix or writing those massive, sprawling update emails to my cousins. The drive to keep producing completely overrides my habit of tracking. And then suddenly, the battery just dies. The crash hits, and I’m right back in that heavy, wet concrete feeling. \n\nSo, I honestly couldn't tell you if it's three nights, or five nights, or just one really long, chaotic stretch that just feels like a week. I just haven't been able to pin down a clear pattern for the exact number of days in a row before it stops. I wish I had a better answer for you, I really do, because not having that data point just feels terribly disorganized on my end.\n\nDoctor: When you come down from one of those high-energy, sleepless stretches and crash back into the heavy, flat feeling—does that crash happen pretty quickly, like within a day or two? And then, how long do you typically stay in that heavy, flat state before the energy and reduced sleep start building up again?\n\nPatient: Oh, the crash. Yes. It's... well, it's incredibly abrupt. It’s not a gentle glide down, like when you're wrapping up a Friday afternoon at the office and just slowly winding down your inbox, clearing out the drafts and making a neat little to-do list for Monday. No, it’s more like... like someone just tripped over the main power cord in the server room and everything goes completely dark all at once. Within a day, or sometimes even just over the course of a single afternoon, I go from feeling like I can manage the entire western seaboard's logistics to just... barely being able to lift my arms to type a simple meeting reminder. It hits fast and it hits so hard. \n\nAnd as for how long I stay down there before the energy comes back... gosh, it's just so difficult to measure the heavy periods because, like I was saying before, time just drags so terribly when you feel that empty. Sometimes it feels like I'm in that thick fog for weeks, just absolutely slogging through the bare minimum at work, doing everything in my power to make sure my partners don't notice that I'm completely hollowed out inside. \n\nBut the thing is, the pattern is just relentless. Because for most of this whole period—really, looking back at the whole timeline—I’ve just been constantly cycling back into those driven, overconfident stretches where I sleep less and take on way too much. I'll be down in the mud, feeling like I can't do anything, and then suddenly I'm right back up, completely convinced I can fix every broken system in my brother's care plan or the firm's entire digital filing structure single-handedly. \n\nEven if some weeks are quieter... you know, I might have a week or two in between where things seem almost manageable, where the dust settles and I'm not firing off massive emails at 4 AM. But inevitably, I just cycle right back into that high-speed gear. It's like my brain simply can't tolerate the quiet for too long before it decides it needs to run the upgraded operating system again. I just keep bouncing back into that state where I'm overconfident, taking on the weight of the world, and running on fumes, over and over again.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those heavy, flat periods—the ones where you feel like you're moving through wet concrete and you've withdrawn from photography and mentoring—roughly how many weeks or months does that typically last before you start to feel the energy and drive building back up again?\n\nPatient: You know, I... I really wish I could give you a neat, tidy number for that, too. I really do. Because, again, my entire professional life is built around timelines. If you ask me how long the Q3 financial review will take, I can tell you it’s going to be exactly three hours and fifteen minutes, with a hard stop for catering at noon. But when it comes to these heavy periods... I have absolutely no idea how many weeks or months it actually is. None at all. \n\nIt’s the exact same problem I have with tracking the high-energy stretches, but almost in reverse. Because when I'm in that state, that... that wet concrete feeling you mentioned, time just entirely loses its meaning. It warps. A single afternoon sitting at my desk, trying to force myself to just sort through a partner's inbox, can feel like an entire month. I'll look at the clock and it's 2:00 PM, and I'll look again and it's 2:03 PM, and I feel like I've aged a decade just trying to drag my hand across the mousepad. \n\nSo when I try to look back and quantify it—when I try to say, \"Okay, was I down in that hole for two weeks? Was it six weeks? Was it two full months?\"—I just draw a complete blank. I try to track it, I really do. I'll open up my calendar and try to see when I stopped going to my photography group, or when I canceled my mentoring sessions, but the days just bleed together into this gray sludge. I might see that I didn't take any photos in April, but does that mean the heavy feeling lasted all of April, or was I just busy? I can't trust my own memory of it because the exhaustion just completely clouds my judgment.\n\nIt’s so incredibly frustrating. I mean, I manage other people's lives down to the minute. I know exactly when my boss needs to leave for the airport to beat the traffic on the I-90, but I can't even tell you if my own brain was shut down for three weeks or eight weeks. I just... I don't have that information. It feels like an absolute eternity while I'm in it, but as for an actual measurement in weeks or months before the energy starts building back up? I simply don't know. I've tried to figure it out, I sit there with my little notebook trying to connect the dots, but the data just isn't there for me to give you a solid answer.\n\nDoctor: Let me try to understand the pattern differently. If we look at a typical month over these past 11 months, how many times would you say you cycle through one of those high-energy, sleepless stretches and then crash back down into the heavy, flat feeling? Is it happening multiple times a month, roughly once a month, or is the pattern more irregular than that?\n\nPatient: Gosh, I... I know this is becoming a theme here, and I apologize, I really do, because I know you're trying to establish a baseline and I'm just not giving you the metrics you need. But... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't know. If you're asking for a neat average, like saying \"oh, it happens exactly twice a month, right around the 15th and the 30th like payroll,\" I just... I don't have that for you. It's so completely irregular. \n\nI mean, I'll sit down with my planner—and I use this very specific color-coding system for my boss's schedule, you know, blue for internal meetings, red for external, green for travel—and I've tried, I really have tried, to apply something like that to my own moods. I'll try to map out these past 11 months to see if there's a \"typical\" month in there somewhere. But there just isn't one. \n\nSometimes it feels like I'm bouncing back and forth constantly, almost like a ping-pong ball. I'll be up, redesigning a matrix or sending out those sprawling emails, then crashing into the mud, and then suddenly I'm right back up a week later planning a massive corporate retreat that hasn't even been approved yet. But then other times, I'll look back and think, well, maybe in October it only happened once? Or maybe I was just in that heavy, wet concrete state for the entire month and the high energy didn't actually hit again until November? \n\nIt completely defies logic. And for someone in my line of work, that is just... it's maddening. My entire professional value is based on my ability to forecast and manage time. If a senior partner asks me how often the steering committee meets, I don't say \"well, it's irregular.\" I say \"the second Tuesday of every month at 10 AM, sharp.\" But with this... with my own mind... the pattern is just that there is no pattern. Or at least, not one that I've been able to track. So, is it multiple times a month? Once a month? I genuinely have no idea. I wish I did, because then I could at least put it in a spreadsheet and brace myself for it, but it just seems to happen entirely on its own chaotic schedule.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Well, see, even though I was saying before about how abrupt it feels—that whole analogy about pulling the power cord in the server room—if you're asking me to actually look back and point to a clear moment or a specific day where I can definitively say, "Yes, that is the exact second the switch flipped," I... I honestly don't know. I really couldn't tell you. 

It’s not like looking at an Outlook calendar. When I schedule a board meeting, there is a definitive start time. The gavel comes down at 9:00 AM, the minutes start being recorded, and there is a clear, indisputable record of when the event began. I have a timestamp. But with this crash... I just don't have a timestamp for it. I don't know if there's a specific moment I can point to, or if the sheer exhaustion of it just makes the transition impossible to remember clearly. 

I've actually tried to sit down and retrace my steps after the fact, you know? I'll think, "Okay, I was feeling fantastic during the morning briefing on Wednesday, I was completely on top of the world, but by the time I was confirming the lunch catering for the executive team... was I already crashing? Was it happening then, or did it happen later when I was sorting the mail?" And I just draw a complete blank. I don't know. It’s like I know the lights went out quickly, but I couldn't tell you what time the clock said when they actually went dark. 

So whether there's a clear, pinpointed moment of transition, or if it's just a really fast, blurry slide down into the mud... I simply don't know. I wish I could give you a clear answer, like "it always switches right after lunch," but I just don't have that level of clarity. The exact moment of the switch is just another missing piece of data that I can't seem to track, no matter how hard I try to monitor myself.

I'm Dr. [Name], a psychiatrist. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Well, hi Dr. [Name]. Thank you for seeing me. I, um... I guess the main reason I’m here—and it’s a little hard to explain because it’s not like a physical pain or a specific event that happened—but what made me finally make the appointment is that the things that usually make me feel like *me* have just... stopped. \n\nI’ve always been a very active, highly organized person. I’m an executive administrative assistant, so keeping things running smoothly and staying on top of schedules is literally my livelihood, you know? I usually pride myself on keeping a perfectly clean calendar, color-coded, everything in its proper place, both at work and in my personal life. I usually love that sense of order. But lately, I don't know, it’s like... well, take my photography walks, for instance. I usually go out every single weekend, rain or shine, usually down by the river or through the historic district, just trying to catch the morning light. I have all this gear I usually love setting up, and it centers me. And mentoring the kids—I volunteer with this local youth group, helping teenagers figure out their plans, which is normally the absolute highlight of my week. \n\nBut recently, all of that... the photography, the mentoring, even just the simple satisfaction of keeping that clean calendar... it all started feeling so incredibly flat. Just flat and heavy. Like trying to move through wet concrete. I just stopped initiating them entirely. I’ll wake up on a Saturday and think about grabbing my camera or heading down to the youth center, and this massive weight just sits on my chest, and I just... don't do it. I end up doing nothing at all. It’s just this pervasive, exhausting heaviness where there used to be passion, and it’s been going on long enough now that my calendar is a mess and I haven't taken a single photo in weeks, which is completely unheard of for me. So, I just really needed to figure out why everything feels so empty right now.\n\nDoctor: When did you first notice this heavy, flat feeling starting to develop? Was there a particular time or event around when it began, or did it come on gradually?\n\nPatient: Well, it's funny you ask about a specific time, because I've actually thought a lot about this. I've been trying to retrace my steps, you know, going back through my old calendars—the ones that are still color-coded—trying to pinpoint the exact moment the color just sort of drained out of everything. And it wasn't like flipping a switch, not exactly. It was more like... well, it’s been about a year and three months now. I know that sounds very specific, but I have a mind for timelines. \n\nIt started right around the time things got really complicated with my family. My brother, actually. His health took a real turn, and his behavior started changing in ways that were... well, they were very disruptive and confusing for everyone. And because I'm the organized one, the one who handles the schedules and the logistics—both at work and in the family—I naturally stepped in to coordinate everything. I figured, okay, I'll just skip the photography walk this one weekend because I spent four hours on the phone with the insurance company on Friday and I just need to sleep. But then one weekend turned into two, and then a month, and then I realized I wasn't just tired from the coordination. It was like the effort of managing his crisis had just slowly siphoned away all the energy I had for my own life. And that was... yeah, about fifteen months ago. That's when I first noticed that the heaviness wasn't lifting even when I did have a free afternoon. I'd sit there with a blank hour on my schedule, which usually feels like a gift, and I'd just stare at the wall.\n\nDoctor: Beyond this heavy, flat feeling you've described, have you been experiencing a lot of worry or anxiety—like racing thoughts, a sense of dread, or feeling on edge—or is it mainly just this lack of energy and emptiness?\n\nPatient: Well, it's... I wouldn't say it's worry, exactly. Or anxiety in the way people usually mean it, like pacing the floors or biting your nails. I don't get that sense of impending doom or dread, you know? It's not like my heart is racing or I'm sweating about what's going to happen tomorrow. I mean, I'm an administrative assistant, so I'm used to anticipating problems and putting out fires before they even start. I'm very good at compartmentalizing stress when it comes to logistics. \n\nBut racing thoughts... that's a tricky one. Because it's not that my mind is racing with anxiety or fear. It's more like... well, it's just this overwhelming emptiness. It's so strange to try and explain, because you'd think emptiness would be quiet, right? Like a vacant room. But for me, it's just this heavy, thick fog. I don't feel on edge at all. If anything, I feel like I'm sinking into the floor. It's just... flat. It's just that complete lack of energy I was talking about. I can sit in my favorite chair, looking at my camera gear, and there's no panic, no worry about *why* I'm not doing it. There's just... nothing. Just this exhausting weight that makes even the idea of worrying seem like it would take too much effort. I don't know if that makes sense, but no, I wouldn't say I'm anxious or having racing thoughts. It's really just the emptiness taking over everything.\n\nDoctor: How has your sleep been over these past 15 months? Are you sleeping more than usual, less than usual, or has it stayed about the same?\n\nPatient: Well, sleep... sleep has actually been one of the most confusing parts of this whole timeline, to be perfectly honest with you. You'd think, given how heavy and flat I feel during the day—like I was saying earlier about trying to move through wet concrete—that I would just be sleeping twelve or fourteen hours a day, right? Just hibernating to escape the weight of it all. But it’s actually... well, it’s been quite the opposite in a really strange, unpredictable way. \n\nI've always been very particular about my sleep hygiene. I usually keep the room at exactly sixty-eight degrees, no screens an hour before bed, because in my line of work, if you're not mentally sharp, you miss a scheduling conflict and an executive's entire week falls apart. But over these past fifteen months, there have been these nights—and I've actually started tracking them in a separate little notebook on my nightstand just to see if I could find a pattern—where I will only sleep for maybe two or three hours. Total. I'll finally drift off around midnight after trying to wind down, and then, boom. My eyes just snap open. \n\nIt'll be 2:30 or 3:00 in the morning, way before dawn, pitch black outside, just completely silent in the neighborhood. And the bizarre thing is, I don't feel groggy at all. I don't have that heavy, dragging feeling that plagues me during the afternoon. I actually wake up feeling completely alert. Instead of feeling tired, like any normal person would on three hours of sleep, I feel totally ready to tackle the day. I’ll literally get out of bed in the dark, make a cup of coffee, and sit down at my computer because I feel completely ready to answer emails, or go through and update all my appointment lists, or reorganize the travel itineraries for the partners I support. I'll just sit there at 3:30 in the morning, knocking out administrative tasks and feeling perfectly energized, which just makes absolutely no sense to me when I think about how exhausted and empty I feel the rest of the time.\n\nDoctor: During those nights when you wake up at 2 or 3 AM feeling alert and energized—do you feel like that energy and productivity level is your normal baseline, or does it actually feel elevated and more energetic than how you usually felt before all of this started 15 months ago?\n\nPatient: Well, elevated is... yes, I suppose elevated is definitely the right word for it. Because, you know, before all this started with my brother fifteen months ago, I was always a very organized, productive person. You really don't survive as an executive administrative assistant if you aren't completely on top of your game and managing your time well. But my normal baseline was always very methodical. Very step-one, step-two, check the list, log off at a reasonable hour. \n\nWhat happens during these 3 AM wake-ups is... it's something else entirely. It's like my brain is suddenly running on a completely upgraded, high-speed operating system, and it's definitely way beyond how I used to operate. \n\nDuring those stretches I start rebuilding work templates from scratch because suddenly the old ones look completely inefficient to me. And I won't just do that—I'll be doing it while simultaneously making these incredibly complex, multi-tab spreadsheet care plans for my brother's medical needs. And then, while I'm bouncing between those two things, I'm emailing relatives, sending these huge, detailed updates to my aunts and cousins at 4:15 in the morning. Which, in hindsight, I know they probably find a bit alarming to wake up to, seeing timestamps like that, but in the moment, I just have to get the information out there. And I'll be reorganizing the shared digital files for the partners I support, renaming everything, color-coding the folders... just doing it all at once. \n\nIt’s this intense, driving pressure, honestly, like sitting still would mean I’m dropping the ball on everything. If I stop typing, if I stop planning and fixing things, it feels like the whole house of cards is just going to completely collapse. So, no, it's definitely not my normal baseline. My normal baseline always had a stopping point, a moment where I could say, \"Okay, the work is done for today.\" But during these nights, there is no stopping point. I just have to keep all the plates spinning at lightning speed.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling that heaviness and flatness during the day—the exhaustion, the lack of interest in photography and mentoring—how long do those stretches typically last before you start to feel better? And separately, how long do those energized, highly productive periods at night tend to go on?\n\nPatient: Well, the heaviness... gosh, it's so hard to put a strict timeline on the emptiness because when you're in it, time just sort of stops. It just feels endless, like it's stretching out forever and you can't even remember what a normal day feels like. \n\nBut those energized periods? The ones where I'm waking up in the pitch dark? I actually have a very specific timeline for those, because, like I said, I document everything. It's the executive assistant in me, I just can't help but track the data and look for the patterns. I was actually looking through my little nightstand notebook just the other day, cross-referencing it with the timestamps on my sent emails—because you can sort Outlook by time of day, which is an absolute lifesaver when you're managing multiple executives across different time zones—and I realized exactly how long this has been going on. \n\nThese elevated, driven stretches... where I'm functioning on practically zero sleep and just bursting with, frankly, way too much confidence about how I can single-handedly organize everyone's lives and solve every logistical crisis... they've been recurring for about eleven months now. Eleven months! I traced it all the way back to late last year. I remember because I had completely redesigned the firm's holiday travel matrix at 3:15 in the morning and felt like an absolute genius doing it. I was so incredibly sure of myself, just running on pure adrenaline and no rest, thinking I had unlocked some secret level of productivity that nobody else knew about. \n\nSo yes, it's been about eleven months that these specific, highly driven, sleepless stretches have been popping up and recurring. It's just wild to look at the paper trail and see it laid out like that, seeing month after month where I have these bursts of just absolute, over-the-top confidence and energy, completely taking over my nights.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those energized, highly productive stretches—like the one where you redesigned the holiday travel matrix at 3 AM—how many nights in a row does that elevated energy and reduced need for sleep typically last before it stops?\n\nPatient: Gosh, you know, that is the one piece of data that absolutely drives me crazy because... well, you'd think with my little nightstand notebook and my obsession with tracking the timestamps on my Outlook sent folder, I would have a precise number for you. A neat little spreadsheet column that says, you know, \"Stretch duration: exactly this many days.\" \n\nBut... I really don't. I don't actually know how many nights in a row it lasts. \n\nIt’s so incredibly frustrating for me as someone whose entire career is built on knowing exactly how long a quarterly board meeting will run, or exactly how many days a senior partner needs on the ground in Tokyo before catching a flight to London. I live for exact durations. But when I’m in one of those stretches... time just sort of loses its normal structure. It’s like the boundaries between, say, Monday night, Tuesday morning, and Wednesday afternoon just completely dissolve into this one continuous, high-speed blur of doing, doing, doing. \n\nI’ll look back at my notes later trying to piece it together, and I might see I was up at 3 AM on a Tuesday, and then there are frantic scribbles from 4 AM on a Wednesday, but sometimes I forget to log things properly because I’m just so caught up in the momentum of it all—like redesigning that travel matrix or writing those massive, sprawling update emails to my cousins. The drive to keep producing completely overrides my habit of tracking. And then suddenly, the battery just dies. The crash hits, and I’m right back in that heavy, wet concrete feeling. \n\nSo, I honestly couldn't tell you if it's three nights, or five nights, or just one really long, chaotic stretch that just feels like a week. I just haven't been able to pin down a clear pattern for the exact number of days in a row before it stops. I wish I had a better answer for you, I really do, because not having that data point just feels terribly disorganized on my end.\n\nDoctor: When you come down from one of those high-energy, sleepless stretches and crash back into the heavy, flat feeling—does that crash happen pretty quickly, like within a day or two? It’s not a gentle glide down, like when you're wrapping up a Friday afternoon at the office and just slowly winding down your inbox, clearing out the drafts and making a neat little to-do list for Monday. No, it’s more like... like someone just tripped over the main power cord in the server room and everything goes completely dark all at once. Within a day, or sometimes even just over the course of a single afternoon, I go from feeling like I can manage the entire western seaboard's logistics to just... barely being able to lift my arms to type a simple meeting reminder. It hits fast and it hits so hard. \n\nAnd as for how long I stay down there before the energy comes back... gosh, it's just so difficult to measure the heavy periods because, like I was saying before, time just drags so terribly when you feel that empty. Sometimes it feels like I'm in that thick fog for weeks, just absolutely slogging through the bare minimum at work, doing everything in my power to make sure my partners don't notice that I'm completely hollowed out inside. \n\nBut the thing is, the pattern is just relentless. Because for most of this whole period—really, looking back at the whole timeline—I’ve just been constantly cycling back into those driven, overconfident stretches where I sleep less and take on way too much. I'll be down in the mud, feeling like I can't do anything, and then suddenly I'm right back up, completely convinced I can fix every broken system in my brother's care plan or the firm's entire digital filing structure single-handedly. \n\nEven if some weeks are quieter... you know, I might have a week or two in between where things seem almost manageable, where the dust settles and I'm not firing off massive emails at 4 AM. But inevitably, I just cycle right back into that high-speed gear. It's like my brain simply can't tolerate the quiet for too long before it decides it needs to run the upgraded operating system again. I just keep bouncing back into that state where I'm overconfident, taking on the weight of the world, and running on fumes, over and over again.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those heavy, flat periods—the ones where you feel like you're moving through wet concrete and you've withdrawn from photography and mentoring—roughly how many weeks or months does that typically last before you start to feel the energy and drive building back up again?\n\nPatient: You know, I... I really wish I could give you a neat, tidy number for that, too. I really do. Because, again, my entire professional life is built around timelines. If you ask me how long the Q3 financial review will take, I can tell you it’s going to be exactly three hours and fifteen minutes, with a hard stop for catering at noon. But when it comes to these heavy periods... I have absolutely no idea how many weeks or months it actually is. None at all. \n\nIt’s the exact same problem I have with tracking the high-energy stretches, but almost in reverse. Because when I'm in that state, that... that wet concrete feeling you mentioned, time just entirely loses its meaning. It warps. A single afternoon sitting at my desk, trying to force myself to just sort through a partner's inbox, can feel like an entire month. I'll look at the clock and it's 2:00 PM, and I'll look again and it's 2:03 PM, and I feel like I've aged a decade just trying to drag my hand across the mousepad. \n\nSo when I try to look back and quantify it—when I try to say, \"Okay, was I down in that hole for two weeks? Was it six weeks? Was it two full months?\"—I just draw a complete blank. I try to track it, I really do. I'll open up my calendar and try to see when I stopped going to my photography group, or when I canceled my mentoring sessions, but the days just bleed together into this gray sludge. I might see that I didn't take any photos in April, but does that mean the heavy feeling lasted all of April, or was I just busy? I can't trust my own memory of it because the exhaustion just completely clouds my judgment.\n\nIt’s so incredibly frustrating. I mean, I manage other people's lives down to the minute. I know exactly when my boss needs to leave for the airport to beat the traffic on the I-90, but I can't even tell you if my own brain was shut down for three weeks or eight weeks. I just... I don't have that information. It feels like an absolute eternity while I'm in it, but as for an actual measurement in weeks or months before the energy starts building back up? I simply don't know. I've tried to figure it out, I sit there with my little notebook trying to connect the dots, but the data just isn't there for me to give you a solid answer.\n\nDoctor: Let me try to understand the pattern differently. If we look at a typical month over these past 11 months, how many times would you say you cycle through one of those high-energy, sleepless stretches and then crash back down into the heavy, flat feeling? Is it happening multiple times a month, roughly once a month, or is the pattern more irregular than that?\n\nPatient: Gosh, I... I know this is becoming a theme here, and I apologize, I really do, because I know you're trying to establish a baseline and I'm just not giving you the metrics you need. But... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't know. If you're asking for a neat average, like saying \"oh, it happens exactly twice a month, right around the 15th and the 30th like payroll,\" I just... I don't have that for you. It's so completely irregular. \n\nI mean, I'll sit down with my planner—and I use this very specific color-coding system for my boss's schedule, you know, blue for internal meetings, red for external, green for travel—and I've tried, I really have tried, to apply something like that to my own moods. I'll try to map out these past 11 months to see if there's a \"typical\" month in there somewhere. But there just isn't one. \n\nSometimes it feels like I'm bouncing back and forth constantly, almost like a ping-pong ball. I'll be up, redesigning a matrix or sending out those sprawling emails, then crashing into the mud, and then suddenly I'm right back up a week later planning a massive corporate retreat that hasn't even been approved yet. But then other times, I'll look back and think, well, maybe in October it only happened once? Or maybe I was just in that heavy, wet concrete state for the entire month and the high energy didn't actually hit again until November? \n\nIt completely defies logic. And for someone in my line of work, that is just... it's maddening. My entire professional value is based on my ability to forecast and manage time. If a senior partner asks me how often the steering committee meets, I don't say \"well, it's irregular.\" I say \"the second Tuesday of every month at 10 AM, sharp.\" But with this... with my own mind... the pattern is just that there is no pattern. Or at least, not one that I've been able to track. So, is it multiple times a month? Once a month? I genuinely have no idea. I wish I did, because then I could at least put it in a spreadsheet and brace myself for it, but it just seems to happen entirely on its own chaotic schedule.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those high-energy stretches where you're awake at 3 AM feeling energized and productive, and then you crash into that heavy, flat feeling—when the crash happens, does it feel like there's a clear moment or day where you can point to and say 'that's when it switched,' or does the transition feel more blurred and gradual?\n\nPatient: Well, see, even though I was saying before about how abrupt it feels—that whole analogy about pulling the power cord in the server room—if you're asking me to actually look back and point to a clear moment or a specific day where I can definitively say, \"Yes, that is the exact second the switch flipped,\" I... I honestly don't know. I really couldn't tell you. \n\nIt’s not like looking at an Outlook calendar. When I schedule a board meeting, there is a definitive start time. The gavel comes down at 9:00 AM, the minutes start being recorded, and there is a clear, indisputable record of when the event began. I have a timestamp. But with this crash... I just don't have a timestamp for it. I don't know if there's a specific moment I can point to, or if the sheer exhaustion of it just makes the transition impossible to remember clearly. \n\nI've actually tried to sit down and retrace my steps after the fact, you know? I'll think, \"Okay, I was feeling fantastic during the morning briefing on Wednesday, I was completely on top of the world, but by the time I was confirming the lunch catering for the executive team... was I already crashing? Was it happening then, or did it happen later when I was sorting the mail?\" And I just draw a complete blank. I don't know. It’s like I know the lights went out quickly, but I couldn't tell you what time the clock said when they actually went dark. \n\nSo whether there's a clear, pinpointed moment of transition, or if it's just a really fast, blurry slide down into the mud... I simply don't know. I wish I could give you a clear answer, like \"it always switches right after lunch,\" but I just don't have that level of clarity. The exact moment of the switch is just another missing piece of data that I can't seem to track, no matter how hard I try to monitor myself.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Instead of feeling tired, like any normal person would on three hours of sleep, I feel totally ready to tackle the day. I’ll literally get out of bed in the dark, make a cup of coffee, and sit down at my computer because I feel completely ready to answer emails, or go through and update all my appointment lists, or reorganize the travel itineraries for the partners I support. I'll just sit there at 3:30 in the morning, knocking out administrative tasks and feeling perfectly energized, which just makes absolutely no sense to me when I think about how exhausted and empty I feel the rest of the time.\n\nDoctor: During those nights when you wake up at 2 or 3 AM feeling alert and energized—do you feel like that energy and productivity level is your normal baseline, or does it actually feel elevated and more energetic than how you usually felt before all of this started 15 months ago?\n\nPatient: Well, elevated is... yes, I suppose elevated is definitely the right word for it. Because, you know, before all this started with my brother fifteen months ago, I was always a very organized, productive person. You really don't survive as an executive administrative assistant if you aren't completely on top of your game and managing your time well. But my normal baseline was always very methodical. Very step-one, step-two, check the list, log off at a reasonable hour. \n\nWhat happens during these 3 AM wake-ups is... it's something else entirely. It's like my brain is suddenly running on a completely upgraded, high-speed operating system, and it's definitely way beyond how I used to operate. \n\nDuring those stretches I start rebuilding work templates from scratch because suddenly the old ones look completely inefficient to me. And I won't just do that—I'll be doing it while simultaneously making these incredibly complex, multi-tab spreadsheet care plans for my brother's medical needs. And then, while I'm bouncing between those two things, I'm emailing relatives, sending these huge, detailed updates to my aunts and cousins at 4:15 in the morning. Which, in hindsight, I know they probably find a bit alarming to wake up to, seeing timestamps like that, but in the moment, I just have to get the information out there. And I'll be reorganizing the shared digital files for the partners I support, renaming everything, color-coding the folders... just doing it all at once. \n\nIt’s this intense, driving pressure, honestly, like sitting still would mean I’m dropping the ball on everything. If I stop typing, if I stop planning and fixing things, it feels like the whole house of cards is just going to completely collapse. So, no, it's definitely not my normal baseline. My normal baseline always had a stopping point, a moment where I could say, \"Okay, the work is done for today.\" But during these nights, there is no stopping point. I just have to keep all the plates spinning at lightning speed.\n\nDoctor: When you're feeling that heaviness and flatness during the day—the exhaustion, the lack of interest in photography and mentoring—how long do those stretches typically last before you start to feel better? And separately, how long do those energized, highly productive periods at night tend to go on?\n\nPatient: Well, the heaviness... gosh, it's so hard to put a strict timeline on the emptiness because when you're in it, time just sort of stops. It just feels endless, like it's stretching out forever and you can't even remember what a normal day feels like. \n\nBut those energized periods? The ones where I'm waking up in the pitch dark? I actually have a very specific timeline for those, because, like I said, I document everything. It's the executive assistant in me, I just can't help but track the data and look for the patterns. I was actually looking through my little nightstand notebook just the other day, cross-referencing it with the timestamps on my sent emails—because you can sort Outlook by time of day, which is an absolute lifesaver when you're managing multiple executives across different time zones—and I realized exactly how long this has been going on. \n\nThese elevated, driven stretches... where I'm functioning on practically zero sleep and just bursting with, frankly, way too much confidence about how I can single-handedly organize everyone's lives and solve every logistical crisis... they've been recurring for about eleven months now. Eleven months! I traced it all the way back to late last year. I remember because I had completely redesigned the firm's holiday travel matrix at 3:15 in the morning and felt like an absolute genius doing it. I was so incredibly sure of myself, just running on pure adrenaline and no rest, thinking I had unlocked some secret level of productivity that nobody else knew about. \n\nSo yes, it's been about eleven months that these specific, highly driven, sleepless stretches have been popping up and recurring. It's just wild to look at the paper trail and see it laid out like that, seeing month after month where I have these bursts of just absolute, over-the-top confidence and energy, completely taking over my nights.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those energized, highly productive stretches—like the one where you redesigned the holiday travel matrix at 3 AM—how many nights in a row does that elevated energy and reduced need for sleep typically last before it stops?\n\nPatient: Gosh, you know, that is the one piece of data that absolutely drives me crazy because... well, you'd think with my little nightstand notebook and my obsession with tracking the timestamps on my Outlook sent folder, I would have a precise number for you. A neat little spreadsheet column that says, you know, \"Stretch duration: exactly this many days.\" \n\nBut... I really don't. I don't actually know how many nights in a row it lasts. \n\nIt’s so incredibly frustrating for me as someone whose entire career is built on knowing exactly how long a quarterly board meeting will run, or exactly how many days a senior partner needs on the ground in Tokyo before catching a flight to London. I live for exact durations. But when I’m in one of those stretches... time just sort of loses its normal structure. It’s like the boundaries between, say, Monday night, Tuesday morning, and Wednesday afternoon just completely dissolve into this one continuous, high-speed blur of doing, doing, doing. \n\nI’ll look back at my notes later trying to piece it together, and I might see I was up at 3 AM on a Tuesday, and then there are frantic scribbles from 4 AM on a Wednesday, but sometimes I forget to log things properly because I’m just so caught up in the momentum of it all—like redesigning that travel matrix or writing those massive, sprawling update emails to my cousins. The drive to keep producing completely overrides my habit of tracking. And then suddenly, the battery just dies. The crash hits, and I’m right back in that heavy, wet concrete feeling. \n\nSo, I honestly couldn't tell you if it's three nights, or five nights, or just one really long, chaotic stretch that just feels like a week. I just haven't been able to pin down a clear pattern for the exact number of days in a row before it stops. I wish I had a better answer for you, I really do, because not having that data point just feels terribly disorganized on my end.\n\nDoctor: When you come down from one of those high-energy, sleepless stretches and crash back into the heavy, flat feeling—does that crash happen pretty quickly, like within a day or two? And then, how long do you typically stay in that heavy, flat state before the energy and reduced sleep start building up again?\n\nPatient: Oh, the crash. Yes. It's... well, it's incredibly abrupt. It’s not a gentle glide down, like when you're wrapping up a Friday afternoon at the office and just slowly winding down your inbox, clearing out the drafts and making a neat little to-do list for Monday. No, it’s more like... like someone just tripped over the main power cord in the server room and everything goes completely dark all at once. Within a day, or sometimes even just over the course of a single afternoon, I go from feeling like I can manage the entire western seaboard's logistics to just... barely being able to lift my arms to type a simple meeting reminder. It hits fast and it hits so hard. \n\nAnd as for how long I stay down there before the energy comes back... gosh, it's just so difficult to measure the heavy periods because, like I was saying before, time just drags so terribly when you feel that empty. Sometimes it feels like I'm in that thick fog for weeks, just absolutely slogging through the bare minimum at work, doing everything in my power to make sure my partners don't notice that I'm completely hollowed out inside. \n\nBut the thing is, the pattern is just relentless. Because for most of this whole period—really, looking back at the whole timeline—I’ve just been constantly cycling back into those driven, overconfident stretches where I sleep less and take on way too much. I'll be down in the mud, feeling like I can't do anything, and then suddenly I'm right back up, completely convinced I can fix every broken system in my brother's care plan or the firm's entire digital filing structure single-handedly. \n\nEven if some weeks are quieter... you know, I might have a week or two in between where things seem almost manageable, where the dust settles and I'm not firing off massive emails at 4 AM. But inevitably, I just cycle right back into that high-speed gear. It's like my brain simply can't tolerate the quiet for too long before it decides it needs to run the upgraded operating system again. I just keep bouncing back into that state where I'm overconfident, taking on the weight of the world, and running on fumes, over and over again.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those heavy, flat periods—the ones where you feel like you're moving through wet concrete and you've withdrawn from photography and mentoring—roughly how many weeks or months does that typically last before you start to feel the energy and drive building back up again?\n\nPatient: You know, I... I really wish I could give you a neat, tidy number for that, too. I really do. Because, again, my entire professional life is built around timelines. If you ask me how long the Q3 financial review will take, I can tell you it’s going to be exactly three hours and fifteen minutes, with a hard stop for catering at noon. But when it comes to these heavy periods... I have absolutely no idea how many weeks or months it actually is. None at all. \n\nIt’s the exact same problem I have with tracking the high-energy stretches, but almost in reverse. Because when I'm in that state, that... that wet concrete feeling you mentioned, time just entirely loses its meaning. It warps. A single afternoon sitting at my desk, trying to force myself to just sort through a partner's inbox, can feel like an entire month. I'll look at the clock and it's 2:00 PM, and I'll look again and it's 2:03 PM, and I feel like I've aged a decade just trying to drag my hand across the mousepad. \n\nSo when I try to look back and quantify it—when I try to say, \"Okay, was I down in that hole for two weeks? Was it six weeks? Was it two full months?\"—I just draw a complete blank. I try to track it, I really do. I'll open up my calendar and try to see when I stopped going to my photography group, or when I canceled my mentoring sessions, but the days just bleed together into this gray sludge. I might see that I didn't take any photos in April, but does that mean the heavy feeling lasted all of April, or was I just busy? I can't trust my own memory of it because the exhaustion just completely clouds my judgment.\n\nIt’s so incredibly frustrating. I mean, I manage other people's lives down to the minute. I know exactly when my boss needs to leave for the airport to beat the traffic on the I-90, but I can't even tell you if my own brain was shut down for three weeks or eight weeks. I just... I don't have that information. It feels like an absolute eternity while I'm in it, but as for an actual measurement in weeks or months before the energy starts building back up? I simply don't know. I've tried to figure it out, I sit there with my little notebook trying to connect the dots, but the data just isn't there for me to give you a solid answer.\n\nDoctor: Let me try to understand the pattern differently. I know this is becoming a theme here, and I apologize, I really do, because I know you're trying to establish a baseline and I'm just not giving you the metrics you need. But... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't know. If you're asking for a neat average, like saying \"oh, it happens exactly twice a month, right around the 15th and the 30th like payroll,\" I just... I don't have that for you. It's so completely irregular. \n\nI mean, I'll sit down with my planner—and I use this very specific color-coding system for my boss's schedule, you know, blue for internal meetings, red for external, green for travel—and I've tried, I really have tried, to apply something like that to my own moods. I'll try to map out these past 11 months to see if there's a \"typical\" month in there somewhere. I honestly don't know. I really couldn't tell you. \n\nIt’s not like looking at an Outlook calendar. When I schedule a board meeting, there is a definitive start time. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I... well, I was actually just trying to figure that out in the waiting room before they called me in. I really was. I was sitting there, scrolling back through my phone, looking at my sent emails, trying to see when the 3 AM timestamps stopped. Because I know I was up late recently working on this massive, incredibly detailed itinerary for the regional directors—I was color-coding all the flight layovers and the hotel transfers, and I felt completely wired, like I didn't need a single ounce of sleep and could have probably run a marathon right there in my living room. 

But if you're asking me to tell you whether that was this week, or this month, or further back... I honestly, I just don't know. I really can't give you a straight answer. 

It all just blurs together so terribly. I mean, my boss will ask me, "When did we meet with the software vendors from Chicago?" and I can tell him without even blinking, "April 12th at 2:30 PM in Conference Room B, and we ordered the turkey wraps." I have that locked down. But my own brain? My own energy levels? It's like the filing cabinet in my head has just been dumped upside down on the floor. I'll try to piece it together and think, "Okay, maybe the high energy ended this past weekend, because I felt so completely sluggish and heavy on Sunday." But then I'll remember that I was up reorganizing my entire kitchen pantry and scrubbing the baseboards at midnight on Monday. So did it end? Did it just pause? Was that a different week entirely?

It is so incredibly frustrating to not have a grasp on this. As someone who literally lives and breathes timelines and schedules for a living, it is genuinely mortifying to sit here and tell you that I have absolutely no idea when my own most recent episode ended. I wish I could look at you and say, "Yes, it ended last Thursday at dinnertime," but I just... I don't know. I can't separate this week from last month when it comes to tracking my own state of mind. The dates are just completely lost to me.

I'll try to map out these past 11 months to see if there's a \"typical\" month in there somewhere. I'll be up, redesigning a matrix or sending out those sprawling emails, then crashing into the mud, and then suddenly I'm right back up a week later planning a massive corporate retreat that hasn't even been approved yet. But then other times, I'll look back and think, well, maybe in October it only happened once? Or maybe I was just in that heavy, wet concrete state for the entire month and the high energy didn't actually hit again until November? \n\nIt completely defies logic. Or at least, not one that I've been able to track. So, is it multiple times a month? Once a month? I genuinely have no idea. I wish I did, because then I could at least put it in a spreadsheet and brace myself for it, but it just seems to happen entirely on its own chaotic schedule.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those high-energy stretches where you're awake at 3 AM feeling energized and productive, and then you crash into that heavy, flat feeling—when the crash happens, does it feel like there's a clear moment or day where you can point to and say 'that's when it switched,' or does the transition feel more blurred and gradual?\n\nPatient: Well, see, even though I was saying before about how abrupt it feels—that whole analogy about pulling the power cord in the server room—if you're asking me to actually look back and point to a clear moment or a specific day where I can definitively say, \"Yes, that is the exact second the switch flipped,\" I... I just don't have a timestamp for it. The exact moment of the switch is just another missing piece of data that I can't seem to track, no matter how hard I try to monitor myself.\n\nDoctor: Let me ask this a different way. I'll try to piece it together and think, \"Okay, maybe the high energy ended this past weekend, because I felt so completely sluggish and heavy on Sunday.\" But then I'll remember that I was up reorganizing my entire kitchen pantry and scrubbing the baseboards at midnight on Monday. So did it end? Did it just pause? Was that a different week entirely?\n\nIt is so incredibly frustrating to not have a grasp on this. As someone who literally lives and breathes timelines and schedules for a living, it is genuinely mortifying to sit here and tell you that I have absolutely no idea when my own most recent episode ended. I wish I could look at you and say, \"Yes, it ended last Thursday at dinnertime,\" but I just... I don't know. Right now? Honestly, sitting right here in this chair... I feel like I'm wearing one of those lead aprons they put over your chest at the dentist. It's definitely that flat, heavy feeling. I don't feel a drop of that 3 AM electric energy today. 

And honestly, doctor, that heavy feeling is exactly what made me finally pick up the phone and come in to see you. Because it’s started bleeding into absolutely everything. It’s one thing when I can't track my moods for work, but it’s gotten to the point where the things that usually make me feel like *me*—the things that give me a sense of identity outside of being an executive assistant—have just started feeling incredibly flat and heavy, and I just... I stopped initiating them entirely. 

Take my photography walks, for instance. I usually love taking my camera down to the botanical gardens or the waterfront on Sunday mornings. The light is just perfect right around 7 AM, you know? It catches the dew on the leaves and creates this gorgeous depth of field, and it's usually my time to just breathe and be creative. But lately, I’ll look at my camera bag sitting by the door, and it just feels like it weighs a thousand pounds. I can't even bring myself to unzip it, let alone walk out the door. 

And it's not just the photography. I do this mentoring program with some of the older kids in my neighborhood—helping them with college applications, organizing their community service hours, making sure their resumes are formatted correctly. I usually thrive on it. I love seeing them succeed and get their acceptance letters. But recently, the thought of organizing a meetup or even just sending a text to check in on their essays just felt so unbelievably exhausting. I just stopped reaching out. I stopped initiating the sessions. 

Even my own calendar—which, as you know by now, is basically my lifeline—has just fallen by the wayside. Keeping a clean, perfectly color-coded calendar usually brings me so much peace of mind. But my personal planner is just sitting on my desk at home, completely blank for the last couple of weeks. No blue ink, no red ink, no sticky notes. Nothing. It’s just... flat. Everything is just flat. And for me to just abandon the things that define who I am, to just stop doing the very things that bring me joy and structure because the effort feels like moving mountains... well, that’s when I knew I couldn't just keep trying to push through it on my own. Because right now, even just sitting here explaining it to you, I just feel totally weighed down by that wet concrete.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. I’m an executive administrative assistant, so keeping things running smoothly and staying on top of schedules is literally my livelihood, you know? I usually pride myself on keeping a perfectly clean calendar, color-coded, everything in its proper place, both at work and in my personal life. I usually love that sense of order. But lately, I don't know, it’s like... well, take my photography walks, for instance. I usually go out every single weekend, rain or shine, usually down by the river or through the historic district, just trying to catch the morning light. I have all this gear I usually love setting up, and it centers me. And mentoring the kids—I volunteer with this local youth group, helping teenagers figure out their plans, which is normally the absolute highlight of my week. \n\nBut recently, all of that... the photography, the mentoring, even just the simple satisfaction of keeping that clean calendar... it all started feeling so incredibly flat. Just flat and heavy. Like trying to move through wet concrete. I just stopped initiating them entirely. I’ll wake up on a Saturday and think about grabbing my camera or heading down to the youth center, and this massive weight just sits on my chest, and I just... don't do it. I end up doing nothing at all. No, no... I want to be really clear about that. Right now, as I'm sitting here talking to you, it is purely the heaviness. There is absolutely none of that electric, driven energy mixed in today. I mean, if there were, you would definitely know it, and I would certainly feel it. 

Because when I actually have that energy... well, let me tell you what that actually looks like, just so you have the complete picture of the contrast here. During those stretches, I don't just feel awake—I am in constant, unending motion. I start rebuilding all of our department's work templates from scratch, I'm talking rewriting the macros, adjusting the letterheads, standardizing the fonts across fifty different documents so everything is perfectly uniform. And while I'm doing that, I'm simultaneously making these incredibly detailed care plans for my aging aunt, and I'm emailing relatives on the other side of the country that I haven't spoken to in months, and I'm reorganizing years of shared server files... and I am doing all of this all at once. 

It's this overwhelming, undeniable pressure to just produce and fix and arrange. It feels like sitting still would mean I’m dropping the ball on my entire life, you know? Like if I pause for even one second, the whole operation is going to fall apart. That's what the electric energy feels like. It's productive, but it's relentless.

But today? Right now? I couldn't even imagine opening a spreadsheet. The mere thought of drafting a simple email to my sister makes me want to put my head down on this desk and sleep for a week. I don't have the drive to organize a single paperclip right now. So, to answer your question, no, it's not a mix at all today. It's just the flat, heavy, dragging feeling. Just the wet concrete. Nothing else.

It's just wild to look at the paper trail and see it laid out like that, seeing month after month where I have these bursts of just absolute, over-the-top confidence and energy, completely taking over my nights.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those energized, highly productive stretches—like the one where you redesigned the holiday travel matrix at 3 AM—how many nights in a row does that elevated energy and reduced need for sleep typically last before it stops?\n\nPatient: Gosh, you know, that is the one piece of data that absolutely drives me crazy because... well, you'd think with my little nightstand notebook and my obsession with tracking the timestamps on my Outlook sent folder, I would have a precise number for you. A neat little spreadsheet column that says, you know, \"Stretch duration: exactly this many days.\" \n\nBut... I really don't. I don't actually know how many nights in a row it lasts. \n\nIt’s so incredibly frustrating for me as someone whose entire career is built on knowing exactly how long a quarterly board meeting will run, or exactly how many days a senior partner needs on the ground in Tokyo before catching a flight to London. I live for exact durations. But when I’m in one of those stretches... time just sort of loses its normal structure. It’s like the boundaries between, say, Monday night, Tuesday morning, and Wednesday afternoon just completely dissolve into this one continuous, high-speed blur of doing, doing, doing. \n\nI’ll look back at my notes later trying to piece it together, and I might see I was up at 3 AM on a Tuesday, and then there are frantic scribbles from 4 AM on a Wednesday, but sometimes I forget to log things properly because I’m just so caught up in the momentum of it all—like redesigning that travel matrix or writing those massive, sprawling update emails to my cousins. The drive to keep producing completely overrides my habit of tracking. And then suddenly, the battery just dies. The crash hits, and I’m right back in that heavy, wet concrete feeling. \n\nSo, I honestly couldn't tell you if it's three nights, or five nights, or just one really long, chaotic stretch that just feels like a week. I just haven't been able to pin down a clear pattern for the exact number of days in a row before it stops. I wish I had a better answer for you, I really do, because not having that data point just feels terribly disorganized on my end.\n\nDoctor: When you come down from one of those high-energy, sleepless stretches and crash back into the heavy, flat feeling—does that crash happen pretty quickly, like within a day or two? And then, how long do you typically stay in that heavy, flat state before the energy and reduced sleep start building up again?\n\nPatient: Oh, the crash. Yes. It's... well, it's incredibly abrupt. It’s not a gentle glide down, like when you're wrapping up a Friday afternoon at the office and just slowly winding down your inbox, clearing out the drafts and making a neat little to-do list for Monday. No, it’s more like... like someone just tripped over the main power cord in the server room and everything goes completely dark all at once. Within a day, or sometimes even just over the course of a single afternoon, I go from feeling like I can manage the entire western seaboard's logistics to just... barely being able to lift my arms to type a simple meeting reminder. It hits fast and it hits so hard. \n\nAnd as for how long I stay down there before the energy comes back... gosh, it's just so difficult to measure the heavy periods because, like I was saying before, time just drags so terribly when you feel that empty. Sometimes it feels like I'm in that thick fog for weeks, just absolutely slogging through the bare minimum at work, doing everything in my power to make sure my partners don't notice that I'm completely hollowed out inside. \n\nBut the thing is, the pattern is just relentless. Because for most of this whole period—really, looking back at the whole timeline—I’ve just been constantly cycling back into those driven, overconfident stretches where I sleep less and take on way too much. I'll be down in the mud, feeling like I can't do anything, and then suddenly I'm right back up, completely convinced I can fix every broken system in my brother's care plan or the firm's entire digital filing structure single-handedly. \n\nEven if some weeks are quieter... you know, I might have a week or two in between where things seem almost manageable, where the dust settles and I'm not firing off massive emails at 4 AM. But inevitably, I just cycle right back into that high-speed gear. It's like my brain simply can't tolerate the quiet for too long before it decides it needs to run the upgraded operating system again. I just keep bouncing back into that state where I'm overconfident, taking on the weight of the world, and running on fumes, over and over again.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those heavy, flat periods—the ones where you feel like you're moving through wet concrete and you've withdrawn from photography and mentoring—roughly how many weeks or months does that typically last before you start to feel the energy and drive building back up again?\n\nPatient: You know, I... I really wish I could give you a neat, tidy number for that, too. I really do. Because, again, my entire professional life is built around timelines. If you ask me how long the Q3 financial review will take, I can tell you it’s going to be exactly three hours and fifteen minutes, with a hard stop for catering at noon. But when it comes to these heavy periods... I have absolutely no idea how many weeks or months it actually is. None at all. \n\nIt’s the exact same problem I have with tracking the high-energy stretches, but almost in reverse. Because when I'm in that state, that... that wet concrete feeling you mentioned, time just entirely loses its meaning. It warps. A single afternoon sitting at my desk, trying to force myself to just sort through a partner's inbox, can feel like an entire month. I'll look at the clock and it's 2:00 PM, and I'll look again and it's 2:03 PM, and I feel like I've aged a decade just trying to drag my hand across the mousepad. \n\nSo when I try to look back and quantify it—when I try to say, \"Okay, was I down in that hole for two weeks? Was it six weeks? Was it two full months?\"—I just draw a complete blank. I try to track it, I really do. I'll open up my calendar and try to see when I stopped going to my photography group, or when I canceled my mentoring sessions, but the days just bleed together into this gray sludge. I might see that I didn't take any photos in April, but does that mean the heavy feeling lasted all of April, or was I just busy? I can't trust my own memory of it because the exhaustion just completely clouds my judgment.\n\nIt’s so incredibly frustrating. I mean, I manage other people's lives down to the minute. I know exactly when my boss needs to leave for the airport to beat the traffic on the I-90, but I can't even tell you if my own brain was shut down for three weeks or eight weeks. I just... I don't have that information. It feels like an absolute eternity while I'm in it, but as for an actual measurement in weeks or months before the energy starts building back up? I simply don't know. I've tried to figure it out, I sit there with my little notebook trying to connect the dots, but the data just isn't there for me to give you a solid answer.\n\nDoctor: Let me try to understand the pattern differently. If we look at a typical month over these past 11 months, how many times would you say you cycle through one of those high-energy, sleepless stretches and then crash back down into the heavy, flat feeling? Is it happening multiple times a month, roughly once a month, or is the pattern more irregular than that?\n\nPatient: Gosh, I... I know this is becoming a theme here, and I apologize, I really do, because I know you're trying to establish a baseline and I'm just not giving you the metrics you need. But... I honestly couldn't tell you. I just don't know. If you're asking for a neat average, like saying \"oh, it happens exactly twice a month, right around the 15th and the 30th like payroll,\" I just... I don't have that for you. It's so completely irregular. \n\nI mean, I'll sit down with my planner—and I use this very specific color-coding system for my boss's schedule, you know, blue for internal meetings, red for external, green for travel—and I've tried, I really have tried, to apply something like that to my own moods. I'll try to map out these past 11 months to see if there's a \"typical\" month in there somewhere. But there just isn't one. \n\nSometimes it feels like I'm bouncing back and forth constantly, almost like a ping-pong ball. I'll be up, redesigning a matrix or sending out those sprawling emails, then crashing into the mud, and then suddenly I'm right back up a week later planning a massive corporate retreat that hasn't even been approved yet. But then other times, I'll look back and think, well, maybe in October it only happened once? Or maybe I was just in that heavy, wet concrete state for the entire month and the high energy didn't actually hit again until November? \n\nIt completely defies logic. And for someone in my line of work, that is just... it's maddening. My entire professional value is based on my ability to forecast and manage time. If a senior partner asks me how often the steering committee meets, I don't say \"well, it's irregular.\" I say \"the second Tuesday of every month at 10 AM, sharp.\" But with this... with my own mind... the pattern is just that there is no pattern. Or at least, not one that I've been able to track. So, is it multiple times a month? Once a month? I genuinely have no idea. I wish I did, because then I could at least put it in a spreadsheet and brace myself for it, but it just seems to happen entirely on its own chaotic schedule.\n\nDoctor: When you're in one of those high-energy stretches where you're awake at 3 AM feeling energized and productive, and then you crash into that heavy, flat feeling—when the crash happens, does it feel like there's a clear moment or day where you can point to and say 'that's when it switched,' or does the transition feel more blurred and gradual?\n\nPatient: Well, see, even though I was saying before about how abrupt it feels—that whole analogy about pulling the power cord in the server room—if you're asking me to actually look back and point to a clear moment or a specific day where I can definitively say, \"Yes, that is the exact second the switch flipped,\" I... I honestly don't know. I really couldn't tell you. \n\nIt’s not like looking at an Outlook calendar. When I schedule a board meeting, there is a definitive start time. I just don't have a timestamp for it. I don't know if there's a specific moment I can point to, or if the sheer exhaustion of it just makes the transition impossible to remember clearly. \n\nI've actually tried to sit down and retrace my steps after the fact, you know? I'll think, \"Okay, I was feeling fantastic during the morning briefing on Wednesday, I was completely on top of the world, but by the time I was confirming the lunch catering for the executive team... was I already crashing? Was it happening then, or did it happen later when I was sorting the mail?\" And I just draw a complete blank. I don't know. Over the past 11 months, if you think back to the last time you were in one of those high-energy, sleepless stretches—the most recent one—when did that actually end? Was it this week, this month, or further back?\n\nPatient: I... well, I was actually just trying to figure that out in the waiting room before they called me in. I really was. I was sitting there, scrolling back through my phone, looking at my sent emails, trying to see when the 3 AM timestamps stopped. Because I know I was up late recently working on this massive, incredibly detailed itinerary for the regional directors—I was color-coding all the flight layovers and the hotel transfers, and I felt completely wired, like I didn't need a single ounce of sleep and could have probably run a marathon right there in my living room. \n\nBut if you're asking me to tell you whether that was this week, or this month, or further back... No blue ink, no red ink, no sticky notes. Nothing. It’s just... flat. Everything is just flat. Because right now, even just sitting here explaining it to you, I just feel totally weighed down by that wet concrete.\n\nDoctor: You mentioned feeling that heavy, flat, wet concrete feeling right now as you're sitting here with me. It’s actually bizarre when I really stop and think about it, because normally, you know, if I don't get my standard seven or eight hours, I am just a complete zombie the next day at the office. I need my coffee, I need a slow morning, I can barely string a sentence together. \n\nBut during those stretches? It's like my physical body just completely bypasses the biological need for rest. I will have these nights—and this has happened so many times over the last year—where I will maybe, maybe get two or three hours of sleep. Tops. I'll finally force myself to lay down in bed at midnight, just staring at the ceiling, and then by 2:30 or 3 AM, way before dawn, my eyes just snap open. And you would think I'd be absolutely exhausted, right? You'd think I'd be dragging. But I don't feel groggy at all. I don't have that heavy-eyed, sand-in-your-eyes feeling. \n\nInstead of feeling tired, I feel this intense, immediate readiness. It’s like someone flipped a breaker switch in my chest. I'll literally roll right out of bed in the pitch dark, walk out to the kitchen, and I don't even need to brew coffee because I'm already buzzing. I'll just open up my laptop right there on the kitchen island and I am instantly ready to start answering emails. I'll be firing off replies to our overseas vendors, drafting memos, or I'll pull up the master schedules and just start updating the appointment lists for the senior vice presidents. \n\nI mean, I'll be sitting there at 3:15 in the morning, just the glow of the screen lighting up the room, cross-referencing three different Outlook calendars, fixing double-bookings, moving meetings around, and I feel completely refreshed. Like I just slept for a week! It’s just... my brain decides that sleep is completely optional, and frankly, just a total waste of time when there are schedules to optimize. So yes, I definitely experience that. Just two or three hours, waking up in the dark, and I'm totally fine with it. I'm ready to conquer the inbox."
